A second interview noted Captain Malcolm G. Armstrong relayed that his brother, 1st Lt. E. B. Armstrong, had seen a formation of unexplainable objects near Lake Mead heading south at about 10,000 feet. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 211–212](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 136](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 167–168](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, p. 48](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, June 30** — 9:45 a.m. On 30 June 1947 at about 09:45 local time near Boise/Voice, Idaho, a witness and his wife observed a single aerial object about 10 miles away and roughly 3,000 feet up. The object was seen for a few minutes, appeared to cling to a haze or home cloud, and was described as half-circular and like a mirror reflecting sunlight before disappearing. The witness reported Aircraft Recognition training in the USAF and stated it was not an airplane. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 52–55](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, June 30** — 4:30 p.m. On 30 June at 16:30 near Seligman, Arizona, a single witness reported a dull grey circular object about 30 degrees above the horizon. It was seen for about 8 seconds, appeared roughly 1.5 inches in diameter at an estimated 10,000 feet altitude, and was said to travel at 2,000 mph or faster before disappearing in the distance. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_006, p. 47](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_6.pdf>))

**1947, June 30** — 5:45 p.m. On 30 June 1947 at about 5:45 PM, Angelo Donofrio reported seeing a single bright, silvery half-circled object near Boise, Idaho while traveling west toward the Plantation Golf Course with his wife. He estimated it was about 10 miles away, around 3,000 feet altitude, appearing stationary and seemingly clinging to a huge cloud. After a few minutes, it disappeared. He stated he had prior aircraft-recognition training in the USAF and was positive it was not an airplane. ([38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 44–45](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, June** — In June 1947, Harold A. Dahl and Fred Crisman reported picking up strange rock-like fragments from a gravel pit on Maury Island, Washington, and mailing a cigar box of samples to Ray Palmer for analysis. The report describes later phone calls, press interest, and a July 31, 1947 meeting with Kenneth Arnold, but it does not document a direct visual UAP sighting; it mainly concerns alleged debris associated with the flying disc stories. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_003, p. 51](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_3.pdf>))

**1947, June** — In June 1947, Kenneth Arnold, flying a private plane near Mt. Rainier, reported seeing nine distant, saucerlike objects moving along the mountain slopes at very high apparent speed. The source text argues that turbulent mountain inversion haze, warped layers, and sunlight reflections could explain the sighting as a mirage-like optical effect rather than a true craft. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_006, p. 259](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_6.pdf>))

**1947, July 1** — An FBI memorandum from Knoxville summarized Colonel C. D. Gasser's review of July 1947 photographs reportedly taken by William Presley near Oak Ridge, Tennessee, of an unidentified object appearing to fly over the area. Technical evaluation by Air Materiel Command concluded the images were not genuine flying saucer photos but instead showed film defects consistent with a drop of warm water or developer and possible hoaxing. Gasser nevertheless stated that his earlier views on the existence of flying saucers remained unchanged. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_004, pp. 109–113](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_4.pdf>))

**1947, July 1** — On July 1, 1947, reputable citizens reportedly saw multiple flying discs in the vicinity of Trail Creek near Sun Valley, Idaho. The source is an FBI memorandum dated July 3, 1947, but it contains no direct witness statements or descriptive details beyond the general claim that many people saw the objects. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 97](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 3** _(cluster ×3)_ — 2:30 p.m. John F. Cole, a reliable astronomer in South Brooksville, Maine, reported seeing an unusual group of very light-colored objects on 1947-07-03 at about 14:30 local time, moving generally northwest and accompanied by a loud roar. He estimated roughly 10 objects bunched loosely together with irregular, swarm-like motion and no regular formation; two dark shapes on the left edge may have been tail wings. He speculated they could have been jets, bombers, rockets, or other aircraft from a naval demonstration, but was not certain. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 209–210](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 165–166](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, pp. 63–64](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** _(cluster ×2)_ — 11:00 a.m. On 4 July 1947 at about 11:00, C. J. Bogne of Tigard, Oregon reported that four disc-shaped objects flew past Mt. Jefferson while he was driving near Redmond, Oregon, observing them from the ground. ([DOW-UAP-D087, p. 44](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, p. 35](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** — 11:45 a.m. On 4 July 1947 at about 11:45 a.m. local time, Marshall West Berlin reported seeing a single luminous object east over West Trenton, New Jersey, while standing on the southbound platform of the West Trenton station. He described it as a fast-moving flying saucer with no tail or halo, apparently at about 2100 altitude, under bright moonlight, and leaving no exhaust trail. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 56–59](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** _(cluster ×2)_ — 1:05 p.m. On 4 July 1947 at about 13:05 in Portland, Oregon, patrolman and former Air Corps pilot Earl E. Patterson observed a single disc-shaped object over the city while getting out of his car near Southeast 82nd and Foster Road. He described it as aluminum in color, apparently metallic or eggshell white, flying southwest over Portland at terrific speed around 30,000 feet, leaving no vapor or smoke trail and making no sound. He reported an abrupt 90-degree directional change without difficulty and believed it may have been radio-controlled. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 21–24](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 15–16](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** _(cluster ×2)_ — 1:05 p.m. On 4 July 1947 at 13:05 in Portland, Oregon, Harbor Pilot Capt. K. A. Prehn and two other harbor patrol members reported seeing a corroborated group of three to six shiny chromium objects high over the Globe Mills moving south at terrific speed. The objects oscillated and wobbled, repeatedly disappearing and reappearing, and appeared in changing forms described as a full disc, half-moon shape, then nothing. Witnesses insisted the objects were not planes; a plane was also present in the sky at the time. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 27–30](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 23–24](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** _(cluster ×2)_ — 1:05 p.m. On 4 July 1947 in Portland, Oregon, Patrolman Kenneth A. McDowell reported seeing five large, round disc-shaped objects in the air east of Portland while on duty at Precinct #1 and feeding pigeons in the parking lot behind the police station. He said the pigeons became excited, the objects moved at great speed, with two flying south and three heading east, and all dipped up and down in an oscillating motion. No sound, color, propulsion, or detailed construction could be determined, and the discs disappeared quickly before further observation. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 17–20](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 13–14](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** _(cluster ×4)_ — 1:05 p.m. On 4 July 1947 at 13:05, Patrolman W. A. Lissy of the Portland City Police Department, a private pilot, observed three white, flat round disc-shaped objects from the ground over Portland, Oregon. The objects were estimated at about 40,000 ft, moved at terrific speed in a straight-line formation, and the last disc fluttered rapidly in a sideward arc. No sound or exhaust/vapor/smoke trails were seen. The account was corroborated by Patrolman D. W. Ellis, also a private pilot. ([DOW-UAP-D087, p. 23](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 19–20](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** _(cluster ×2)_ — 1:05 p.m. On 4 July 1947 at about 13:05, Vancouver, Washington Sheriff's Deputy John Sullivan and fellow deputies Clarence McKay and Fred Krives stepped outside after hearing a Portland police radio broadcast and observed 20 to 30 objects over Portland, roughly 3 to 5 miles away. The objects were described as "V" shaped, like a flock of geese, and were accompanied by a low humming sound. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 38–39](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 29–30](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** _(cluster ×3)_ — 1:05 p.m. On 4 July 1947 at 13:05, Oregon State Police Sergeant Claude Cross observed three disc-shaped objects from the police station in Milwaukee, Oregon. The objects were seen at an undetermined altitude traveling northwest at very high speed, following one another in formation. Cross reported a whitish-brown glint from reflected sunlight, no sound, and could not determine their size, speed, or altitude beyond noting the objects' terrific pace. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 19–22](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 17–18](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 184](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** — 1:06 p.m. On 1947-07-04 in Portland, Oregon, multiple police officers and deputy sheriffs received radio reports of disk-like objects over the city and independently observed several fast-moving, flash-emitting objects at high altitude. Patrolmen Walter Lissy and Robert Ellis reported three objects over Oaks Park; Patrolman Earl Patterson saw one object moving southwest; harbor patrol personnel including Capt. K. A. Prehn, A. T. Austed, and K. C. Hoff also saw the objects over the Globe Mills area. Witnesses emphasized the objects were not airplanes, heard no engine noise, and described erratic course changes and oscillating visibility. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 182](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** _(cluster ×2)_ — 2:00 p.m. On 4 July 1947 at about 14:00 in Portland, Oregon, E. A. Evans observed a fast-moving disc-shaped object from the ground; within 2 to 4 seconds it moved out of sight. The report states one disc was seen at first and later two more, with all three resembling metallic discs glinting in sunlight and traveling west-to-east or north as they first appeared in the west and disappeared in the east. ([DOW-UAP-D087, p. 40](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 31–32](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** _(cluster ×2)_ — 4:30 p.m. On 4 July 1947 at about 16:30, Mrs. Lawrence J. Hayward reported from ground level in Portland, Oregon, seeing one small silvery round disc over the Sandy district. She described it as like a new disc flipping around in the air, moving slowly, apparently metallic, with no sound reported. ([DOW-UAP-D087, p. 42](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, p. 33](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** _(cluster ×2)_ — 5:00 p.m. On 4 July 1947 at about 17:00 in Portland, Oregon, witness Thomas W. Dwyer reported seeing three silver objects at high altitude. One object was said to be heading southeast while the other two were moving northeast. No additional details on size, sound, duration, or construction were provided. ([DOW-UAP-D087, p. 43](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, p. 34](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** _(cluster ×2)_ — 8:04 p.m. On 4 July 1947 at 8:04 p.m., UAL pilot Capt. E. J. Smith and the crew of a westbound United Air Lines flight from Boise toward Seattle reported seeing nine unidentified disc-like objects near Emmett/Bennett, Idaho, initially five in a loose formation followed later by four more; the stewardess Marty Morrow was brought to the cockpit and also saw them. The objects were silhouetted against the sunset, followed the aircraft northwest for about 45 miles, and then disappeared, with the witnesses unable to determine whether they outsped the plane or disintegrated. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 34–35](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 25–26](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** — 8:04 p.m. On 1947-07-04/05, multiple witnesses in Oregon and Washington reported flying disks/saucer-like objects. The main account came from United Airlines captain E. J. Smith, who said he saw five thin, smooth-bottomed, rough-topped objects in the sunset shortly after takeoff near Noise, Idaho, and tracked them northwest for about 45 miles before they disappeared. Separate reports came from a Coast Guardsman near Seattle who photographed a white saucer over Lake Washington, a Portland amusement park employee who saw several spinning disks, and Vancouver, Washington sheriff's deputies who observed 20 to 30 goose-like objects over Portland and heard a low hum. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 178](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** — 8:12 p.m. On 4 July 1947, United Air Lines pilots Capt. E. J. Smith and co-pilot Ralph Stevens reported multiple flying-disk-like objects while en route from Boise, Idaho, toward Pendleton/Seattle. Stevens first noticed an apparent aircraft-like object shortly after departure near Emmet, Idaho; the two men then observed groups of four or five objects that appeared to fly in formation, merge or disappear, reappear in a second group, and continue climbing before fading into the sunset. The witnesses described flat-bottomed objects with rough tops, no discernible color due to silhouetting, and estimated speeds around 300 mph. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, pp. 108–111](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** — 10:30 p.m. On the evening of July 4 at about 10:30 PM, the witness said he saw a flying saucer while sitting on his porch. He reported it was at the same apparent level as two visible searchlight beams near the air field by Lake St. Clair, and wondered whether trucks with proper equipment could have created the same illusion. He described two clearly outlined beams and said the saucer appeared when a third beam faded from the ground up to the object. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 16–18](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** — 11:45 p.m. On 4 July 1947 at 11:45 p.m., Miss Marion Marshall reported seeing one luminous flying-saucer-shaped object traveling east over West Trenton, New Jersey, while she stood on the southbound platform of the West Trenton Reading Railroad station. She described it as high, fast, and having no tail or halo, with bright moonlight at the time. ([38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 48–49](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** — A Fourth of July newspaper-style summary notes twelve reports of 'saucers' in widely separated parts of the United States, including one in Trenton, New Jersey that was later traced to a fireworks display. Dr. Paul Fitts, a psychologist associated for a time with Project Saucer, interpreted the cluster as mass suggestibility rather than evidence of genuine craft. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_007, p. 76](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_7.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** — On 4 July 1947 in the evening at Lake City, Seattle, Washington, Coast Guardsman Frank Ryman and his wife watched a single white saucer- or ball-shaped object for about 10 minutes as it moved in horizontal flight over the north end of Lake Washington. Ryman photographed it, and the resulting image reportedly showed only a tiny light spot against the dark sky when the disc was directly overhead. ([38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 27–28](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** — On July 4, 1947, L.C.A. Hoersch reported seeing one of the early 'flying discs' near the dance hall at Asbury Park, New Jersey. He initially thought it might be a balloon, but said it moved in a definite direction at a constant speed while gradually ascending, following a path like a more pronounced bullet trajectory, then deviating farther away north along the street line. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 76](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** — Captain Smith of United Airlines was interviewed at Boise Municipal Airport on 12 July 1947 after reiterating his account of seeing flying disks on the late evening of 4 July 1947, about eight minutes out of Boise on a flight route to Seattle, Washington. The report is based on his earlier press statements and an investigator's opinion that his position made him unlikely to report the event lightly. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_002, p. 174](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_2.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** — On July 4, a Seattle coastguard photographed a small white oblong object described as a "flying saucer" against a dark grey background. The report notes that the U.S. Navy suggested the image resembled its new wingless "Flying Pancake" aircraft, while a meteorologist proposed solar reflections on low cloud as an explanation. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_SUB_A, p. 91](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_sub_a.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** — A July 5, 1947 Associated Press newspaper article summarized numerous flying-saucer reports across North America, including multiple independent civilian and pilot sightings. The relevant incident text describes James Harris, a farmer at Shea-brooke, Prince Edward Island, and his hired man Herman Linkletter reporting one of the disc-like objects traveling from northwest to southeast; the article frames these as part of the wider nationwide wave of reports rather than as a single investigated case. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_SUB_A, p. 122](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_sub_a.pdf>))

**1947, July 4** — Multiple July 1947 UFO reports are summarized in a press article: a Coast Guardsman near Seattle said a picture was taken of a saucer-like object; a United Airlines pilot and co-pilot near Boise chased a strange object for 15 miles; a Boise correspondent and three others saw a white disc overhead for only a few seconds; Portland police and a Washington deputy sheriff reported groups of discs over the Portland area; and a Philadelphia physician described a dark saucer-like object with a luminous halo and whirling-jets appearance. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_SUB_A, p. 124](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_sub_a.pdf>))

**1947, July 5** _(cluster ×2)_ — 2:00 p.m. Multiple daytime sightings of disk-like objects were reported over Portland, Oregon, on July 5, 1947, including several witnesses describing metallic or silver disks moving very fast, one appearing to flip like a dime and move slowly, and another report of silvered disks being dropped from a light airplane over Eugene later that day. ([DOW-UAP-D087, p. 31](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 177](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 5** — 7:00 p.m. On July 5, 1947, in Washington, D.C., two women shopping on Minnesota Avenue NE reported seeing three bright golden objects overhead after hearing a noise like a jet-propelled plane. They described them as dish-shaped, fast as a jet plane, far enough away that size could not be estimated, and disappearing over the horizon. The report was treated as an early authenticated city-limits sighting amid widespread public and scientific debate about flying saucers. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_SUB_A, p. 123](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_sub_a.pdf>))

**1947, July 5** — 8:45 p.m. On the night of July 5, Mrs. Noah Atkinson and her husband observed a very bright, lamp-like object from their front porch in Brookhaven, Georgia, described as a huge lit floor lamp near the treetops and moving very fast across the sky; the article also notes two airline pilots saw a similar flaming object near Montgomery and that multiple witnesses elsewhere reported varied luminous descriptions, though the report itself leans toward mundane explanations. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_004, p. 182](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_4.pdf>))

**1947, July 6** _(cluster ×3)_ — 1:45 p.m. Major Archie B. Browning reported that on 6 July 1947, while flying a B-25 from Ogden, Utah, to Kansas City, Missouri, he saw a very bright, silvery, round disc low off his left side. He estimated it at 10 miles away at first, then about 1 to 2 miles off his left wing at roughly 11 o'clock and around 11,000 ft, traveling in the same direction and at the same speed until he began to turn toward it, when it disappeared. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 232–233](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 187–188](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, pp. 112–113](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 6** — 5:30 p.m. On 1947-07-06 at about 5:30 PM near Darlington, South Carolina, J. U. Watts Jr. reported that about 12 so-called flying discs passed overhead. The report was sent by Western Union to the FBI and later forwarded internally for liaison to the War Department; no further descriptive details of the objects were provided. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 42–44](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 6** _(cluster ×2)_ — 8:45 p.m. On 6 July 1947 at about 20:45 in Birmingham, Alabama, Staff Sergeant Ira L. Livingston, a pilot-rated S/Sgt with 250 hours of flying and armorer-gunner experience, observed what he and a neighbor described as 'flying discs.' He reported seven to ten round objects, about 2 feet in diameter, appearing in the west and traveling southeast at an estimated 500 to 600 mph at roughly 2000 feet above the horizon on a 45-degree angle. The discs were silent, seemed to emit a dim glow or single light, and moved in an arc rather than a straight line; as one disappeared, another would appear behind it, though Livingston said he never saw more than one at a time. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 162–163](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 121–122](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 6** — 8:45 p.m. On the night of 6 July 1947 in Birmingham, Alabama, numerous residents reported seeing mysterious flying discs or light spots moving across the sky, with one Air Corps staff sergeant, Ira L. Livingston, providing a sworn statement describing multiple silent, round, dimly glowing objects traveling southwest to southeast at high speed in a definite arc. The Air Reserve Training Detachment at Birmingham forwarded a photograph and witness statement up the chain, while the Fourteenth Air Force and Air Defense Command merely forwarded the report for information and stated no investigation had been made because it was considered an isolated case. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, pp. 5–14](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 6** _(cluster ×3)_ — On July 6, 1947, while waiting alone in a parked Hollywood Tarzana Bus at the Tarzana end of the line near Ventura Boulevard in Los Angeles, the witness saw a series of uniform, saucer-like objects appear suddenly from the west and move rapidly northward parallel to Ventura Boulevard. Immediately after focusing on the objects, the witness noticed a second phenomenon in the sky between herself and the saucers: milky white crisscrossing rays converging around spinning red, sharp, irregular fragments described as cornelian-like, as if attracted to a magnet. The saucers then went out of sight, and the witness later speculated the rays may have been emitted from the red fragments or from the saucers themselves. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 226–227](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 181–182](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, pp. 52–53](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 6** _(cluster ×2)_ — Captain James H. Burniston reported that while at Fairfield-Suisun Army Air Base with his wife on Sunday, July 6, he observed a fast-moving object traveling northwest to southeast at an estimated 10,000 feet or higher. The object was visible for about sixty seconds, appeared roughly C-54 sized, had no discernible shape, color, wings, or fuselage, rolled side to side three times, flashed in sunlight at moments, and then faded from view at about 30 degrees above the horizon. He could not identify any sound apart from background aircraft noise. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 132–133](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, p. 114](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 6** — AP Science Editor Howard Blakeslee published a July 6, 1947 newspaper article arguing that many flying saucer reports were optical illusions caused by human eyesight, reflected sunlight, distance effects, airplanes, birds, meteors, ice crystals, and suggestion; the piece cites the writer's own observations of round, bright objects over Long Island Sound that later proved to be airplanes. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 55](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 6** — A July 1947 newspaper report relayed a letter alleging that a Russian tanker officer described Soviet atom-powered supersonic planes and controlled radioactive clouds; the piece was investigated by federal agents after an Examiner referral and an atomic scientist said the claims were 'not all nonsense,' but the article itself does not document a direct UAP sighting by an observer. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 54](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 6** — On July 6, 1947, at Grafton, Wisconsin, Catholic priest Joseph Brasky reported hearing a swishing/whirring noise followed by a thud and mild explosion in his parish yard, where he found a warm sheet-metal disc with wiring and gadgets in a central hole; the Associated Press/Washington Post story framed it amid the 1947 flying-saucer wave and noted FBI notification. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 2–4](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 6** — Press report dated July 6, 1947, describing widespread U.S. reports of mysterious 'flying saucers' since June 25; military aircraft, including Oregon National Guard P-51s and a P-80 jet at Muroc Army Air Field, were alerted to watch for the objects, but the sortie reports were negative and no firsthand object was recorded in this specific article. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 51](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** _(cluster ×2)_ — 9:00 a.m. On 7 July 1947 at about 0900 hours near Hickam Village #2/Hickam Villages, Mr. Daniel Sito of the Air Communications Sections at Hickam Field observed a silver, spherical object resembling a large balloon with a silver disc suspended beneath it. The object revolved, flashed as sunlight struck the lower disc, moved slowly northwest toward the sea while ascending to roughly 5,000-6,000 feet, and disappeared into the clouds after a few minutes. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 217–218](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 173–174](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** _(cluster ×3)_ — 10:10 a.m. On 7 July 1947 at about 10:10, Major J. C. Wise, a test pilot at Muroc Army Air Field, saw other personnel looking up and observed an object to the north at an estimated 10,000-12,000 feet. He first thought it was a weather balloon, but after watching it he described a yellowish-white sphere moving west to east at roughly 200-225 mph while oscillating in a forward whirling motion without losing altitude. He had no time to pursue it in a P-80. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 15–16](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 9–10](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_003, p. 68](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_3.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** _(cluster ×2)_ — 10:45 a.m. This FBI office memorandum does not document an actual UAP sighting; instead, it records that press reports about a supposed flying disc in Milwaukee turned out to concern a circular saw allegedly reported by Reverend Joseph Brasky. SAC Johnson said Brasky had not contacted the Milwaukee Office, and the Associated Press believed the item was not being released as a flying-saucer story because it was only a circular saw, photographed with wires and tubes attached and thought to be a prank. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 24](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — 11:45 a.m. On 7 July 1947, the Wisconsin Wing Headquarters of the Civil Air Patrol reported two daytime flying-saucer observations by four witnesses in two airplanes over southeastern Wisconsin. In the first, an instructor and student near Koshkonong/Elkhorn saw a saucer at about 4000 feet MSL that reportedly descended vertically edgewise through clouds, then leveled off and covered 25 miles in 15 seconds. In the second, Capt. R. J. Southey and a passenger near Eagle/East Troy saw a similar object travel from Eagle to Muskego, cover 22 miles in 20 seconds, disappear, reappear farther along its path, and vanish again. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, p. 115](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** _(cluster ×2)_ — 1:00 p.m. On 7 July 1947, while walking on U.S. Highway 92 about 1 mile east of Lakeland, Florida, Mr. Hiram William Griffin observed five shiny, glittery objects ascend rapidly from roughly 5,000 to 7,500 feet in 15-30 seconds. He heard a swishing sound like a shrill whine and thought the objects were about a mile away. The objects were seen at ground level, appeared wingless and possibly 'jet jobs,' with a plastic-looking leading object towing the other four; they climbed out of sight in clear weather with scattered clouds. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 223–224](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 179–180](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — 3:00 p.m. On 1947-07-07 at about 3 PM in West Rindge, New Hampshire, several people sitting on a porch observed a little curl of smoke on Early Whitehead's lawn; inspection found small burned spots and, in dry grass along both sides of the road in a roughly 200-foot circle, several small blazes that triggered a fire department response. Metallic fragments were reportedly recovered from the scene and turned over to MIT scientists, who said the material resembled lining from V-2 bombs or possibly jet turbine aircraft parts; the investigators treated the matter as classified and suggested possible military interest. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 106–108](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** _(cluster ×2)_ — 4:00 p.m. On 7 July 1947 at about 16:00 in Phoenix, Arizona, William Albert Rhodes photographed a single grey elliptical object seen at roughly 5,000 ft, descending spirally to about 2,000 ft, then climbing away at a 45-degree angle while moving about 400-600 MPH from north/northeast toward the west. The object was reported as about 20-30 ft across, difficult to see against cumulus clouds, silent while in view despite a jet-like noise heard beforehand, and showed no propellers or landing gear; Rhodes took two photographs during the sighting. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 119–120](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 88–89](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — 5:00 p.m. On July 7, 1947, near the witness's home and hobby shop at 4333 N. 14th Street in Phoenix, Arizona, William Albert Rhodes reported seeing an odd-shaped aerial object after a storm, photographing it twice as it made a tight spiral turn and then banked right into clouds. He estimated the object at 20 to 30 feet in diameter, about 1,000 to 2,000 feet high at one point, and moving around 100 mph; he said it resembled the cover illustration of Mechanics Illustrated but appeared to be flying backward. FBI Phoenix later interviewed Rhodes with A-2 representative George Fugate present and accepted his negatives for Army Air Force Intelligence. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_002, pp. 115–119](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_2.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — 5:00 p.m. On 1947-07-07, FBI Dallas relayed a telephonic report from Major Curtan of Eighth Air Force that an object purporting to be a flying disc had been recovered near Roswell, New Mexico. The object was described as hexagonal and suspended by cable from a balloon about twenty feet in diameter, resembling a high-altitude weather balloon with a radar reflector. The disc and balloon were being flown to Wright Field for examination, and no further investigation was conducted by the FBI office. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 70](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — 6:05 p.m. On 1947-07-07 in Shreveport, Louisiana, F. C. Hairston reported seeing a circular disc come from the northwest at about 6:05 PM and land in the 1500 block of Texas Avenue. He retrieved the object at 1554 Texas Avenue and described it as whirling in flight with fire spouting from its sides, then smoking after landing. The object was a thin aluminum disc about 16 inches across with small end coils, copper wires, and a central starter-like component marked 'Made in USA'; Barksdale Field took custody and photographed it before FBI arrival. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 72](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** _(cluster ×2)_ — 10:30 p.m. On 7 July 1947 between 2230 and 2300 in Arlington, Virginia, Lt. Col. James A. Cobb observed a single small, blob-shaped object from a bedroom window at 2807 N. Glebe Road. He estimated it was about three-quarters of a mile away, below 500 feet, and moving southeast at jet-like speed, with a duration of only 2-3 seconds before disappearing behind trees. The object appeared as a cool bright reflected white light, with no red in it, and the observer compared it to moonlight on a clear night; two sketches or photographs were noted in the report. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 215–216](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 171–172](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — 10:30 p.m. On 1947-07-07 at about 10:30 P.M. in Omaha, Nebraska, Fred R. Reibold and at least one family member reported seeing a flaming object drop into the street in front of their house at 2315 Kimbrough Avenue. They described the object as solid, round and disc-like, roughly silver-dollar sized and about 1/4 inch thick, burning with extreme heat. The local newspaper was notified, a reporter collected fragments, and a chemistry professor later identified the residue as ordinary pipe tobacco ash, which the witness rejected as an explanation. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 10–15](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — 10:30 p.m. Lt. Col. James O. Cobb reported that around 2230-2300 EDT on or about 7 July 1947, while at home in Arlington, Virginia, he saw a brief light pass rapidly from the right-hand edge of his window view toward the south and disappear below the trees or horizon. He later described it as a cool bright white light with no tail, smoke, or sparks, moving straight and seeming roughly airplane-sized at low altitude, though he believed it was likely a natural phenomenon rather than a weather balloon or airplane fire. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, pp. 65–68](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** _(cluster ×3)_ — The document concerns a July 7, 1947 Phoenix-area flying disc photograph taken by Dr. W. A. Rhodes and later handled through FBI Phoenix Office liaison with Air Force Intelligence representative George Fugate, Jr. The memo itself does not describe the object in motion in detail, but it records that Rhodes photographed 'flying discs' and later transferred the negatives to the FBI for transmittal to the Air Force. The June 4, 1952 memo was prompted by a media inquiry from Fred Blumenthal on behalf of Drew Pearson. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_006, pp. 180–185](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_6.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_004, pp. 207–212](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_4.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — A July 1947 FBI memorandum records scientist Edwin M. Bailey Jr. of Stamford, Connecticut, offering a speculative theory about flying saucers rather than describing a personal sighting; he cited alleged reports from Italy and other cities and suggested the objects could be radio-controlled atom or germ bombs. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 103–105](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — On July 7, 1947, metal fragments were found at West Rindge, New Hampshire and later examined by MIT and FBI-associated personnel. Spectographic analysis determined the pieces were ordinary cast iron subjected to very high heat, originally thought to be metallic alloy. Investigators considered and rejected a train or locomotive source, and noted the fragments likely came from one hollow cylinder about eight inches in diameter and three-sixteenths of an inch thick. A scientist recalled similar cast-iron cylinders had been used in New Mexico guided missile research, but no firm conclusion linked the material to a missile or any UAP. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_003, pp. 26–28](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_3.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — A Pittsburgh newspaper item reported that Connie Dunbar, age 35, said he saw 'mysterious flying saucers'—but also stated he knew the source of the ones he saw because they were thrown by his wife, Mrs. Bessie Dunbar, during a domestic dispute that later resulted in a divorce. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_SUB_A, p. 113](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_sub_a.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — On 1947-07-07, amid nationwide flying-saucer reports, a Chicago woman said she saw a saucer with legs, while the same news roundup described other alleged disks being investigated and often explained as paper washers, weed seeds, searchlight effects, or hoaxes. The Chicago report is brief and sensational, with no technical details beyond the claimed legs. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 52–53](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — On 7 July 1947 at sunset in Phoenix, Arizona, William Rhoads photographed unidentifiable objects near his home; an FBI agent later obtained the pictures from the Arizona Republic. The objects were said to fly at unconceivable speeds and make three circles in the vicinity of the witness's home, with one image taken as it passed in front of him and another as it turned toward him. The object's height was estimated at 1000 feet. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 172](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — This FBI/War Department correspondence concerns an anonymous July 5, 1947 telegram about 'flying disks' and a July 7, 1947 report of two incidents of flying saucers seen by four witnesses in two airplanes over southern Wisconsin; because the reported locations were said to be near Lily Lake, Illinois, investigators asked the Chicago Field Division to inquire about Richard F. Shaver to see whether he had information about the origin of the objects. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_002, pp. 17–19](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_2.pdf>))

**1947, July 7** — A July 7, 1947 sighting reported by William Rhoads of Phoenix, Arizona, was forwarded through FBI channels after A-2 asked that he be fully interviewed; Rhoads said he saw what he believed were flying discs and reportedly photographed them with a 620 box camera. The memo only concerns referral for interview and enclosure of three photographic prints, not the observation itself. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_003, pp. 87–88](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_3.pdf>))

**1947, July 8** _(cluster ×6)_ — 9:30 a.m. On 8 July 1947 at 09:30, 1st Lt Joseph C. McHenry, Billeting Officer at Muroc AAFld, observed first two and later one silver, disc-like or spherical objects over Muroc Air Field, California. The objects were reported at about 8,000 ft altitude, moving about 300 mph on a northerly heading toward Mojave and performing a tight circle; witnesses included S/Sgt Gerald E. Nauman, T/Sgt Joseph Ruvolo, and Miss Jannette Marie Scotte, with additional personnel later seeing a third object circling over the north end of the field. The report states the sighting was confirmed by other sources and that the motion was too tight to be any known aircraft. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 1–3](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 2–3](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_003, pp. 66–67](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_3.pdf>))

**1947, July 8** _(cluster ×3)_ — 9:45 a.m. On 8 July 1947 at about 09:45 near Muroc AAFld, California, S/Sgt Joseph Ruvolo, the NCO in charge of the Billeting Office, was asked by Lt. McHenry to look at the sky and observed two silver, saucer- or disc-shaped flying objects moving northwest at an estimated 350-400 mph at about 7,500-8,000 ft. He reported no sound, no balloon characteristics, and no hallucination, and the case was later confirmed by other sources. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 2–3](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 6–7](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_003, p. 65](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_3.pdf>))

**1947, July 8** _(cluster ×2)_ — 10:00 a.m. On 8 July 1947 at 10:00 in the morning, S/Sgt Gerald E. Nauman observed three disc-shaped objects over Muroc Army Air Field, California, while on the ground at the base. He first saw two objects traveling northwest at an estimated altitude of 7,000 to 8,000 feet and 300 to 400 mph, reflecting sunlight; a few minutes later, after the first pair disappeared, a third object appeared at the same altitude and performed a very tight circular maneuver while the other two maintained a horizontal course. The witness stated the objects were not aircraft, balloons, or birds, and the case was later confirmed by other sources. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 5–6](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_003, p. 72](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_3.pdf>))

**1947, July 8** — 11:22 a.m. An FBI teletype from New Orleans records a report of a 'flying disc' allegedly found by F. G. Hariston on July 8, 1947, but the Bureau notes the disc was revealed to be a prank. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 8–9](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 8** _(cluster ×3)_ — 11:50 a.m. On 1947-07-08 at about 11:50 in Area #3 on Rogers Dry Lake at Muroc Army Air Field, Captain John Paul Strapp and four other people in an observation truck saw a rounded, white-aluminum/silvery object near a P-82 and A-26 formation during a seat-ejection test. It initially resembled a parachute canopy and was first thought to be a premature ejection, then was seen descending at roughly three times the rate of a later parachute drop while drifting slightly north of due west against the wind toward Mount Wilson. As it lowered, it appeared ovular with two projections on top that may have been fins or knobs, and it seemed to rotate or oscillate slowly. No smoke, flame, engine noise, or other visible propulsion was observed; it was visible for about 90 seconds before being lost behind the mountain tops. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 15–18](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 11–12](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_003, pp. 69–70](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_3.pdf>))

**1947, July 8** _(cluster ×3)_ — 12:00 p.m. On 8 July 1947 at about noon near Muroc Army Air Field, California, Major Richard R. Shoop saw a thin metallic-looking object 5 to 8 miles to the north. He described it as aluminum-colored in the sunlight, about the size of a pursuit airplane but not shaped like a conventional plane. The object moved in an oscillating descent toward near-ground level, then climbed back to a fairly high altitude and slowly moved away; his wife reportedly saw it at the same time. ([DOW-UAP-D087, p. 12](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, p. 8](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_003, p. 64](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_3.pdf>))

**1947, July 8** — 12:00 p.m. On 8 July 1947 at approximately noon, Colonel Gilkey reported from the ground at Murco Army Air Field, California, that he saw one object which he believed at the time was probably paper and not significant enough to mention; he later told Captain Harry D. Black that nothing was seen clearly enough to justify further report. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 13–14](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 8** — 12:00 p.m. On 1947-07-08 around noon in Norfolk, Virginia, 15-year-old Billy Turrentine photographed a large dark object he and the accompanying newspaper story described as a flying disc or flying football, with two smaller objects following behind it. He reported the object moved rapidly from southwest to northeast, appeared very high above the clouds, was accompanied by a white mist or dust-like trail, and made no sound. The FBI later interviewed him on 1947-08-08 and recorded that Army authorities in the Tidewater Area were already aware of the matter, so no further FBI investigation would be conducted. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 157–160](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 8** — 3:50 p.m. On 8 July 1947, 1st Lt. Alvin E. Moorman of ACONG 196 Fighter Squadron reported while flying a routine test mission in a P-51 at 20,000 feet that he saw a flat, light-reflecting object with no visible wings or vertical fin, appearing about the depth of a P-51 at roughly 35,000 feet. It was seen again near 50,000 feet over Mount Baldy, stayed in view about 30 seconds, and could not be kept in sight despite efforts to track it; nearby bases reported no aircraft in the air. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, p. 116](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 8** — On 8 July 1947 at Hamilton Field, California, S/Sgt Edward R. Baker saw three objects moving northeast over the base after noticing several men pointing skyward; two objects were round and light gray and seemed faster than ordinary P-80 flight, while a third appeared to be rolling or rotating and brightening/darkening as it moved. He estimated them at 8,000-10,000 feet and 7-10 miles away, with the objects visible for about four seconds. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_002, p. 176](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_2.pdf>))

**1947, July 8** — The report summarizes the 1947 Roswell incident: a crash and debris recovery were announced by the Roswell base on July 8, then quickly retracted as a weather-balloon explanation by General Ramey. Later witness affidavits and video testimonies alleged extraordinary debris, threats of silence, and even humanoid remains, while the Air Force's 1994 report attributed the debris to the secret Mogul balloon project. ([255_413270_UFO's_and_Defense_What_Should_we_Prepar, pp. 72–73](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/255_413270_ufo's_and_defense_what_should_we_prepare_for.pdf>))

**1947, July 9** _(cluster ×3)_ — 11:15 a.m. On 9 July 1947 between about 11:15 and 11:40 in Grand Falls, Newfoundland, Constable Eric Kearsey reported seeing four round, phosphorescent objects flying side by side at terrific speed in an easterly direction, followed later by a fifth object that moved in a rocking motion; his wife and a family friend independently witnessed the objects, describing them as "flying saucers." ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 117–118](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 86–87](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, pp. 34–36](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 9** _(cluster ×2)_ — 12:17 p.m. A National Guard AT-6 pilot, David N. Johnson, flying between Boise and Meridian, Idaho, saw a small round black object in the left side of his field of view on 9 July 1947 at about 12:17. The object, viewed over a distance of more than a mile for about 10-30 seconds against partly cloudy skies, moved erratically with a slow roll and jerky rising motion, briefly presented its edge and flashed in sunlight, then appeared as a thin black line before rolling out of the top of the maneuver and being lost from view. The witness initially considered it a weather balloon, but weather station confirmation indicated no balloon release for several hours; he also exposed 10 seconds of 8mm motion-picture film and made a sketch of the movement. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 221–222](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 177–178](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 9** _(cluster ×2)_ — 12:17 p.m. On July 9, 1947, David N. Johnson, an experienced pilot on an assignment to find and photograph a flying disc, observed a black, round object while flying an AT-6 near Boise, Idaho, over clouds near the Camas Prairie/Fairfield direction. He first mistook it for a weather balloon, then filmed about 10 seconds of 8mm footage, and watched it roll to edge-on, flash once in sunlight, and perform a partial barrel-roll before disappearing. Other military personnel later reported seeing a similar black object in the same cloud formation from the ground. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, pp. 84–92](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_002, pp. 169–173](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_2.pdf>))

**1947, July 9** _(cluster ×2)_ — 5:15 p.m. On July 9, 1947, Raymond Edward Lane and his wife Laura reported that while walking in a field near Midland, Michigan, they heard a puff-like noise about 100 feet away and saw a white, ball-shaped fire about the size of a bushel basket burning roughly a foot above the ground. The fire died out immediately, and Lane later collected a three-inch area of fused sand for laboratory examination. FBI agents interviewed the Lanes; Raymond gave the same basic account, while Laura was reluctant and indefinite, and Dow Chemical authorities considered the story fantastic but examined the material. The material included sand, a small silver nugget, fused sand with silver droplets, and some low-level radioactive gray material. The Army Air Force Intelligence at Selfridge Field was notified and the material was turned over to them. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_002, pp. 8–10](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_2.pdf>))

**1947, July 9** — 10:30 p.m. On July 9, 1947, a reported 'flying disc' landed and burst into flames in North Hollywood near Radford and Magnolia Streets, later said to have fallen in a woman's garden at 11858 Magnolia Boulevard. Fire department personnel recovered the object and an FBI agent photographed it at the Valley Fire Department in Van Nuys before it was turned over to military intelligence, which concluded it was a hoax. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 94–96](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 9** — 11:30 p.m. On July 9, 1947, a resident agent at Burbank, California reportedly received notice of an aluminum disc about 2 feet in diameter and weighing about 10 pounds that had landed in or near Burbank and burst into flame on landing, allegedly starting a woods fire. The object, described as painted with aluminum paint and containing some sort of radio tube in the center, was held by the resident agent and scheduled to be turned over to military authorities in Los Angeles the next morning. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 85](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 9** — 11:30 p.m. On 1947-07-09 at about 23:30 local time near Grand Falls, Newfoundland, Constable Iric Kearsey reported four egg-shaped, phosphorus-colored discs traveling east in a trail/line formation parallel to the ground, followed about ten minutes later by a single disc moving the same way; the objects were estimated at roughly 30,000 feet, appeared very fast, showed black spots on the tail end, left no smoke trail, and were seen in clear weather. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, p. 118](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 10** _(cluster ×2)_ — 10:00 a.m. On 10 July 1947 at about 1000 hours near Cordroy, Newfoundland, Wm Evans saw one very fast circular object, about the size of a dinner plate, colored between red and yellow with a yellowish tail about a yard long. He said it was much brighter than a shooting star, traveled closer than a meteor, and disappeared almost immediately; he did not think it resembled a plane or shooting star. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 172–173](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 129–130](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 10** — 10:00 a.m. On 10 July 1947 at about 10:00 in Cordroy, Newfoundland, store owner John P. Legge, formerly a foreman at Buchans Mine and a civilian Air Detection Corps member, watched a single bright red circular object in the sky for about 15 seconds. He estimated it at roughly 6,000 feet altitude, moving northwest and then eastward at the speed of a shooting star, with a 15-foot afterglow trail that gave it a cone-shaped appearance. Legge insisted it was not a plane or ordinary shooting star; corroborating witness statements were noted from Wm Evans and Albert Samms. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 170–171](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 10** _(cluster ×2)_ — 3:00 p.m. On 10 July 1947 in the afternoon, Robert W. Leidy, a station mechanic traveling by car after fishing near Pan American Airways at Harmon Field, Newfoundland, observed a single flying object or disc moving at very high speed in a circular path through clouds. He reported a definite bluish streak/trail that cut a clear opening through the cloud layer, leaving sharp edges, and took a Kodachrome photograph. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 58–61](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 50–51](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 10** — 3:30 p.m. On July 10, 1947 at about 3:30 p.m. in Black River Falls, Wisconsin, city engineer Sig Hanson reportedly found a large 17-inch disc that looked as if it might have been made from cardboard painted with silver airplane dope. The object had a tube in the center and a small motor with a propeller attached to the side. Colonel Harry Schafer of the Civilian Air Patrol, after being contacted by the Milwaukee FBI office, opined that it would not be able to fly by itself and said it would be taken to Air Corps Headquarters. The matter became a press inquiry, but the FBI had no custody or control of the disc. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 67](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 10** — 3:30 p.m. An FBI office memorandum from Milwaukee reports a July 10, 1947 find near Black River Falls, Wisconsin, of a disc-shaped contraption about 19 inches in diameter discovered by Sig Hanson on the Jackson County fairgrounds; the item was described as possibly cardboard covered with silver airplane dope, with a small wooden tail, an RCA photo-electric cell or tube, and a small electric motor with propeller, and the case was treated as a possible juvenile-made object rather than a genuine aerial phenomenon. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 169–170](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 10** — 5:00 p.m. On 10 July 1947 at about 17:00, John E. Woodruff, Chief Mechanic for Pan American Airways, observed a single translucent, disk-like object while traveling between Harmon Field and Stephenville Crossing in Newfoundland. Seen for only a split second in very clear weather with scattered clouds, it appeared about the size of a DC-4 or Constellation, flew horizontally to the north-northeast at a terrific rate of speed, opened a path through the clouds, and left a dark bluish trail estimated at 15 to 20 miles long. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 62–65](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 10** — 5:00 p.m. On 10 July 1947 near Harmon Field, Newfoundland, three Pan American/TWA mechanics reported seeing a translucent, wheel-like disc or trail moving at very high speed on a horizontal NNE course about 6 miles SSW of the field, at roughly 8,000-10,000 ft. The object appeared to cut a straight path through scattered clouds, leaving a bluish-black trail 15-20 miles long and opening a gap in the cloud deck; two Kodachrome photographs were reportedly taken of the trail. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, pp. 17–29](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 10** — 8:00 p.m. A classified incoming message from Stephenville, Newfoundland reports one flying disc sighted at 2000Z on 10 July 1947 over Harmon Field. The object was seen at about 10,000 feet on a north-northeast course in clear weather with scattered cumulus. Two named observers, Mr. Merhman (TWA representative) and Mr. Woodruff (FAA representative), first saw it about six miles south-southwest of the field. It was described as silvery, circular like a wagon wheel, roughly the apparent span of a C-54 at altitude, and appeared to cut through clouds while leaving a bluish-black trail about fifteen miles long. Kodachrome photographs were taken and were being developed, with the photos to be forwarded later. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, p. 119](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 10** _(cluster ×3)_ — 10:00 p.m. On 10 July 1947 near Codroy, Newfoundland, multiple civilian witnesses, including John P. Legge, Wm. Evans, and 12-year-old Albert Samms, observed a fast-moving, bright disc-like object traveling from the northwest toward the east at an estimated altitude of about 6,000 feet. The object appeared flame-colored or reddish-yellow, with an afterglow or tail that made it look cone-shaped, and was visible only briefly before disappearing. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 174–175](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 131–132](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, pp. 19–33](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 10** — On 10 July 1947, observer John A. Blevins, Jr. reported seeing a blue-black vapor trail from a moving object while traveling by car over a mountain near Stoneseville Crossing. The object passed through and outside scattered clouds, leaving a clear gas-cut path in the sky like a knife slicing through clouds; no sound, size, or exact object form was stated. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 60–63](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 10** — At a home in North Hollywood, California, Russell Long reported finding a saucer-shaped mechanical device in a geranium bed; he said it had been belching smoke from two exhaust pipes and emitting a blue-white glare, with a radio tube on top, wires to a central plug, and a rudder-like wing. Fire Battalion Chief Wallace E. Newcombe inspected it skeptically and thought it did not appear capable of flight. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_SUB_A, p. 111](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_sub_a.pdf>))

**1947, July 11** — 2:45 a.m. On 1947-07-11 at about 02:45 Mountain Time in Twin Falls, Idaho, Mrs. Fred Hasterbrook heard a noise behind her home and investigated, finding in a neighboring backyard a metallic disc-like object later picked up and taken to the Twin Falls Police Department. The object was described in unusual technical detail as a 30.5-inch saucer-shaped disc with a plastic dome bolted to one side, a metal dome on the other, visible tubes and wiring, burned wiring, and an item marked "Inspected TS." ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 79](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 11** — 9:10 p.m. On 11 Jul at 2110, a single witness at Camp Hood, Texas reported a pale red object to the W by SW about 30 degrees above the horizon. It appeared ball-shaped but not a perfect circle, was about twice as large as the evening star, lasted about 2 seconds, and was described as turning off like a flashlight. The report is brief and provides little additional context. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_006, p. 47](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_6.pdf>))

**1947, July 11** — 10:45 p.m. On the night of July 11, 1947, Alvin B. Parker of Laurel, Maryland reported by phone that a "flying disc" had just landed in his yard and that "the machinery is still buzzing." FBI personnel relayed the report to Army G-2, and within about an hour Sergeant Lonis of the Laurel Police Department examined the object and found it to be a homemade device made from a Gulf Oil sign and the top of a garbage can, painted with wet aluminum paint and fitted with a dry cell battery, flashlight bulb, wires, and a buzzer. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 66–68](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 11** — On 11 July 1947 at Elmendorf Field, Anchorage, Alaska, Col. Bruce H. Perry and Maj. Wm. E. Gayser reported a single round, aluminum-colored object resembling a balloon about three feet in diameter. It was seen at an undetermined altitude moving at great speed from north to south. ([DOW-UAP-D087, p. 121](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>))

**1947, July 12** _(cluster ×2)_ — 2:30 p.m. On 12 July 1947 at Elmendorf Field, Alaska, Major Gramm and several officers observed an object resembling a grayfish balloon, about 10 feet in diameter, flying northwest along the contour of the mountains about 5 miles away. The object was estimated at 1,500 feet altitude, moving about 100 mph and paralleling a Charlie-47 landing into a 20 mph wind; the report notes the observation lasted several minutes. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 122–123](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, p. 120](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 12** — In Twin Falls, Idaho, on July 12, local boys planted a homemade object in a yard that resembled a flying disc closely enough to draw the attention of the FBI, Army intelligence officers, police, and civilian observers. The case was later exposed as a juvenile hoax made from discarded phonograph, radio tube, and electrical parts with a plexiglass dome and metallic sides. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_SUB_A, p. 109](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_sub_a.pdf>))

**1947, July 12** — A July 12 report from Salt Lake City said a "Flying Disc" that had fallen with a thud in the yard of a Twin Falls, Idaho housewife was examined by the FBI and Army at Ft. Douglas near Salt Lake City and found to be a hoax. Army examiners said it was a decorative gadget made by four teenage boys from parts of a rundown juke box, with plexiglass dome, radio tubes, chromium-plated edges, and wires. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_SUB_A, p. 110](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_sub_a.pdf>))

**1947, July 12** — A July 12, 1947 letter from Yoakum, Texas describes "flying of fire" and "fire ball" phenomena over Palestine, Cairo, and around Yoakum, with the writer also claiming unusual electrical effects near the airport and broadcasting station. The account mixes possible sky observations with broad claims about people, priests, and Germans talking with them, and does not provide a clean, single-witness description of a discrete UAP event. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 26–33](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 12** — An FBI memorandum dated 16 July 1947 summarizes a follow-up interview with Idaho Daily Statesman aviation editor David N. Johnson about Kenneth Arnold's 24 June 1947 report of nine flying disks over the Cascade Mountains of Washington. The memorandum itself does not describe Johnson's observed objects in detail, but states he was believed credible and had been assigned to investigate the phenomenon by flying over the northwest area; his sworn statement is referenced as an attachment. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_002, p. 168](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_2.pdf>))

**1947, July 13** — 5:00 a.m. On 1947-07-13 in Grafton, Wisconsin, Father Joseph Brasky reported hearing a loud bang and finding a hot circular steel disk with saw teeth and attached wires on the lawn of St. Joseph's Catholic Church; he and local press linked it to the flying-saucer craze, while other residents in Milwaukee, Janesville, Oshkosh, and Green Bay reported seeing luminous disks or balls in the same period. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 46–47](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 14** — 11:21 a.m. At about 1121Z near Elmendorf Field, Colonel Perry and Major Geyser reported seeing an aluminum-colored object resembling a balloon, roughly three feet in diameter, at an undetermined altitude, moving very fast from north to south. The report was transmitted by the 59th AACS Group at Elmendorf, Alaska and filed as confidential correspondence. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, p. 121](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 15** — 9:42 p.m. On 1947-07-15 at about 9:42 PM in West Seattle, Mrs. T. L. Kremmer reported that a flying disk had landed on a 100-foot area and was burning at 9:00 PM before being immediately extinguished; agents were dispatched, recovered the disk, and Navy bomb/electronics experts examined it, concluding it could not fly. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, pp. 99–101](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 15** — Mrs. Gwynne M. Merchant repeatedly reported second-hand accounts of flying objects seen in New Mexico, including light objects at night reportedly followed by explosions and alleged 'disc-like' objects seen in several areas; the memo records her theories and notes FBI/AEC officials considered her unreliable, but it does not document a direct personal sighting by Merchant. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 132](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 16** — 5:00 a.m. In Wisconsin on 1947-07-16, multiple independent civilian witnesses reported flying discs around Milwaukee, Janesville, and Billy Mitchell Field, while a separate object that struck St. Joseph's Church in Grafton was found to be a hot circular saw with wires and tubes attached and was therefore explained conventionally. Witness reports described saucer-shaped or disc-like objects, some with tails, changing color from gold to silver, flying in formation, bobbing, zig-zagging, and moving at high speed. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Section_001, p. 56](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_section_1.pdf>))

**1947, July 16** — On July 16 in Chicago, large numbers of residents reported mysterious objects in the sky the previous morning. Descriptions ranged from "flying discs" and fast-moving jet planes to silvery globules estimated at 40 to 50 feet long, but the objects were later identified as gas balloons used by the University of Chicago for cosmic ray research. ([65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_SUB_A, p. 102](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_sub_a.pdf>))

**1947, July 20** _(cluster ×3)_ — 12:15 a.m. On 20 July 1947 at 0015Z, four or five silvery-to-reddish flashes were seen about one hour from Sidney, Newfoundland while the witnesses were aboard the Burgeo en route from Sydney, Nova Scotia to Port aux Basques, Newfoundland. The observers did not see a solid object, only evenly spaced flashes about one second apart, appearing at roughly 30 degrees above the horizon at an estimated quarter-mile range and moving rapidly in a generally NNE direction; a corroborating witness on the ship's starboard deck reported a similar display on 15 July 1947 with abrupt course changes. ([DOW-UAP-D087, pp. 164–165](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/061226/release_03/documents/DOW-UAP-D087_US-AirForce_Analysis-of-Flying-Objects-in-the-US_1-100.pdf>); [38_143685_box7_Incident_Summaries_1-100, pp. 123–124](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/38_143685_box7_incident_summaries_1-100.pdf>); [65_HS1-834228961_62-HQ-83894_Serial_130, pp. 21–22](<https://www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/release_1/65_hs1-834228961_62-hq-83894_serial_130.pdf>))

**1947, July 20** — 9:45 a.m. On 1947-07-20 near the North bank of the Potomac River about 22 miles east of Cumberland, Maryland, Edward A.
